### A bounded remainder gives unique division

**Remember:** Keep the remainder between zero and one less than the divisor.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For an integer a and positive integer b, write a = qb + r. Require integers q and r with 0 ≤ r &lt; b. Without that bound, one can move multiples of b between the two terms.

**Problem.** Every integer has one quotient and one remainder on division by any positive integer.

**How the definition helps.** The bound makes the difference of any two allowed remainders too small to be a nonzero multiple of b (our own). Without it, 7 equals both 2·3 + 1 and 3·3 − 2 (our own). Existence still uses the fact that there is a greatest integer at most a/b (our own).

**Paper or page:** [Division Theorem/Positive Divisor/Uniqueness](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Division_Theorem/Positive_Divisor/Uniqueness). It says: “the integers q, r such that a = q b + r and 0 ≤ r &lt; b are unique:”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Common divisors explain Euclid’s algorithm

**Remember:** Taking the remainder preserves all common divisors.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For two nonnegative integers, at least one positive, the greatest common divisor is the largest positive integer dividing both. For two positive integers, prime factorization gives another definition using the smaller exponent of each prime.

**Problem.** Let a and b be nonnegative integers with b &gt; 0. The greatest common divisor of a and b equals that of b and the remainder after dividing a by b.

**How the definition helps.** The common-divisor definition makes each division step preserve exactly the same divisors (our own). If a = qb + r, a divisor of b divides a exactly when it divides r (our own). Each nonzero remainder is smaller than the preceding divisor, so the process stops (our own).

**Paper or page:** [Euclidean Algorithm](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Euclidean_Algorithm). It says: “Thus the GCD of a and b is the value of the variable a after the termination of the algorithm.”

**Sources:**

- [Euclid, Elements, Book VII, Proposition 2, translated by Thomas L. Heath.](https://mathcs.clarku.edu/~djoyce/java/elements/bookVII/propVII2.html)

### Nonnegative integers make divisibility an order

**Remember:** Keep one sign so mutual divisibility means equality.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Put the divisibility relation on the nonnegative integers, including zero. Say a divides b if b = ak for some nonnegative integer k.

**Problem.** Divisibility is a partial order on the nonnegative integers.

**How the definition helps.** Keeping one sign makes mutual divisibility force equality in our own proof. In our own counterexample on all integers, 2 and −2 divide each other but are not equal. The work moves to showing that two positive integer factors can multiply to 1 only if both are 1. In our own proof, zero divides only itself, so it causes no exception to antisymmetry.

**nLab:** [greatest common divisor](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/greatest+common+divisor). It says: “The natural numbers 0, 1, 2, … are partially ordered by the divisibility relation”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Ordering by divisibility makes gcd(0,0) equal 0

**Remember:** Everything divides 0, so 0 sits at the top of the divisibility order.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors), 06B23. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** The greatest common divisor of a and b is the common divisor that every other common divisor divides. Here "greatest" means greatest in the divisibility order, where x comes below y when x divides y. In this order, 0 is the greatest element, because every integer divides 0. So gcd(0,0) = 0.

**Problem.** The theorems are Bézout's identity, which says that gcd(a,b) generates the same ideal as a and b, and the statement that the natural numbers form a complete distributive lattice under gcd and lcm. (A lattice is an order in which every pair has a greatest lower bound and a least upper bound. It is complete when every subset has them.)

**How the definition helps.** Measuring "greatest" by divisibility, not by size, makes gcd(0,0) exist, so the theorems hold for all pairs. If "greatest" means largest in size, gcd(0,0) is undefined, because every integer divides 0. With the divisibility order, gcd(0,0) = 0. Then Bézout's identity holds for all pairs: the ideal generated by 0 and 0 is the ideal (0). The article adds that with gcd(0,0) = lcm(0,0) = 0, the natural numbers form a complete lattice. If 0 is left out, this fails.

**Wikipedia:** [Greatest common divisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_common_divisor). It says: “However, zero is its own greatest divisor if greatest is understood in the context of the divisibility relation, so gcd(0, 0) is commonly defined as 0. This preserves the usual identities for GCD, and in particular Bézout's identity”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Prime exponents give the divisor product

**Remember:** For each prime, the smaller and larger exponents add to the original total.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For positive integers a and b, form their greatest common divisor by taking each prime with its smaller exponent in a and b. For their least common multiple, take each prime with its larger exponent. An absent prime has exponent zero. The usual definitions take the largest common divisor and the smallest positive common multiple.

**Problem.** For positive integers a and b, their greatest common divisor times their least common multiple equals ab.

**How the definition helps.** The exponent definitions reduce the identity to adding the smaller and larger exponents of each prime (our own). Their sum equals the sum of the two original exponents (our own). Unique prime factorization makes the descriptions agree with the usual definitions (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Greatest common divisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_common_divisor). It says: “a × b is the product of their least common multiple and their greatest common divisor”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Reduced fractions constrain rational roots

**Remember:** Cancel common factors before testing a rational root.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors), 12D05. arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Write a rational number as p/q with positive q and no common divisor greater than 1. An unrestricted fraction allows common factors in the numerator and denominator.

**Problem.** Let p/q be a nonzero rational root of an integer polynomial, written in lowest terms. Then q divides the leading coefficient and p divides the constant coefficient.

**How the definition helps.** Lowest terms let us cancel the numerator and denominator from the divisibility tests (our own). Clearing denominators shows that q divides the leading coefficient times a power of p (our own). Since q and p are coprime, q divides the coefficient itself (our own). Likewise, p divides the constant coefficient times a power of q, so p divides the constant coefficient (our own). Without reduction, 2/2 solves x − 1 = 0, yet 2 divides neither coefficient (our own).

**Paper or page:** [Rational Root Theorem](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Rational_Root_Theorem). It says: “the constant term a₀ of P(x) is divisible by p.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Divisible differences make congruence respect sums and products

**Remember:** The differences of the sums and products are still multiples of m.

*True by definition. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11A07 (Congruences; primitive roots; residue systems). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For integers a and b and a positive integer m, a ≡ b modulo m means that m divides a − b. Equivalently, a and b have the same remainder on division by m.

**Problem.** If a ≡ b and c ≡ d modulo m, then a + c ≡ b + d and ac ≡ bd modulo m.

**How the definition helps.** The divisibility definition turns compatibility with arithmetic into closure of multiples of m under addition and multiplication by integers. For sums, (a + c) − (b + d) = (a − b) + (c − d). For products, ac − bd = a(c − d) + d(a − b). So both differences are multiples of m (our own). With the remainder definition, one instead substitutes quotient-and-remainder expressions for the inputs and compares the output remainders. The division algorithm supplies the equivalence of the definitions.

**Wikipedia:** [Modular arithmetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modular_arithmetic). It says: “Congruence modulo m is a congruence relation, meaning that it is an equivalence relation compatible with addition, subtraction, and multiplication.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Only coprime residues return to one

**Remember:** Only residues you can undo can return to one.

*Rules out counterexamples. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11A07 (Congruences; primitive roots; residue systems). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For a modulus m greater than 1, a unit is a residue with a multiplicative inverse. Equivalently, its representatives are coprime to m. Define the order of a unit as the least positive exponent that raises it to 1 modulo m.

**Problem.** A residue has a positive power equal to 1 modulo m exactly when it is a unit.

**How the definition helps.** An invertible residue lets us cancel equal powers until a positive power equals 1. In our own proof, two powers must agree because there are only finitely many residues. Conversely, if a positive power equals 1, the preceding power supplies an inverse. Bézout’s identity shows that a residue has an inverse exactly when it is coprime to the modulus. For example, the positive powers of 2 modulo 4 are 2 and then 0.

**Paper or page:** [Integer has Multiplicative Order Modulo n iff Coprime to n](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Integer_has_Multiplicative_Order_Modulo_n_iff_Coprime_to_n). It says: “Let the multiplicative order of a modulo n exist. Then: a ⟂ n that is, a and n are coprime.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Shared remainders make congruences compatible

**Remember:** Check the modulus both congruences share.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A07 (Congruences; primitive roots; residue systems). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Two residue classes with different moduli are compatible if they agree modulo the greatest common divisor of those moduli. For coprime moduli this condition holds for every pair.

**Problem.** Two congruences with positive moduli have one solution class modulo their least common multiple exactly when their residues are compatible.

**How the definition helps.** Compatibility makes the difference of the two residues an integer combination of the moduli. In our own proof, Bézout’s identity supplies integers u and v with b − a = mu + nv. Then a + mu = b − nv solves both congruences. Any two solutions differ by a multiple of both moduli, hence by a multiple of their least common multiple. Without compatibility, even 0 modulo 4 and 1 modulo 6 have no common solution.

**Wikipedia:** [Chinese remainder theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_remainder_theorem). It says: “has a solution if and only if gcd(nᵢ,nⱼ) divides aᵢ − aⱼ whenever i ≠ j.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Prime moduli limit square roots

**Remember:** Over a prime modulus, two square roots agree up to sign.

*Rules out counterexamples. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11A15 (Power residues, reciprocity). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Use the product test for a prime p greater than 1. If p divides a product, it divides at least one factor. The usual definition instead says that p is greater than 1 and has no positive divisors other than 1 and itself.

**Problem.** For any prime p, a residue has at most two square roots modulo p.

**How the definition helps.** The product test forces every root to agree with a fixed root up to sign. In our own proof, if x and y have the same square, then p divides (x − y)(x + y). Thus y equals x or −x modulo p. Modulo 8, the four residues 1, 3, 5, and 7 all square to 1. Euclid’s lemma gives the product test from the usual divisor definition of a prime.

**Wikipedia:** [Vieta's formulas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vieta%27s_formulas). It says: “the quadratic polynomial P(x) = x²−1 has four roots (1, 3, 5, and 7)”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### The Jacobi symbol gives quadratic reciprocity without factoring

**Remember:** Jacobi multiplies the Legendre symbols over the prime factors of the bottom number.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove.* MSC 11A15 (Power residues, reciprocity). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let a be any integer, and let n = p₁⋯pₖ be an odd positive integer, where the primes need not be different. The Jacobi symbol (a/n) is the product of the Legendre symbols (a/p₁)⋯(a/pₖ). The Legendre symbol (a/p) is 0 when the prime p divides a. Otherwise it is 1 when a is a square modulo p, and −1 when a is not.

**Problem.** Quadratic reciprocity holds for all odd, coprime, positive m and n. This gives a fast algorithm for (a/p), similar to the Euclidean algorithm.

**How the definition helps.** The Jacobi symbol lets you flip the symbol without first factoring the top number. With only the Legendre symbol, you can flip (a/p) to (p/a) only when a is prime, so you must factor a first (our own remark). The Jacobi symbol extends the symbol to odd composite bottom numbers. Its reciprocity law follows directly from the definition and the Legendre case. So the article's algorithm computes the symbol in O(log a log b) steps without factoring, like the Euclidean algorithm. The price is a loss of meaning: (a/n) = 1 no longer means that a is a square modulo n.

**Wikipedia:** [Jacobi symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobi_symbol). It says: “The following facts, even the reciprocity laws, are straightforward deductions from the definition of the Jacobi symbol and the corresponding properties of the Legendre symbol.”

**Sources:**

- [F. Lemmermeyer, Reciprocity Laws: From Euler to Eisenstein, Springer (2000)](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-12893-0)

### The Legendre symbol turns quadratic reciprocity into one formula

**Remember:** (a/p) is a sign that says whether a is a square mod p.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11A15 (Power residues, reciprocity). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let p be an odd prime. The Legendre symbol (a/p) is 1 if a is a nonzero square modulo p. It is −1 if a is not a square modulo p, and 0 if p divides a.

**Problem.** Quadratic reciprocity says this: for distinct odd primes p and q, (p/q)·(q/p) = (−1) to the power ((p−1)/2)·((q−1)/2). In words, the product is −1 exactly when p and q both leave remainder 3 on division by 4.

**How the definition helps.** In our own reading, the symbol helps because it is a number, ±1, so you can multiply it, and the law becomes one formula. Gauss wrote aRp and aNp for the same fact (a is a residue, or a non-residue, of p). Also in our own reading, Gauss's notation is a statement and not a number, so you cannot multiply it. With it, the law is a list of cases by the remainders of p and q on division by 4. Legendre introduced his symbol while he tried to prove reciprocity. The symbol is completely multiplicative in a, so it is a Dirichlet character (a completely multiplicative function of a that depends only on a modulo a fixed number). Proving the law is still the hard part.

**Wikipedia:** [Legendre symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legendre_symbol). It says: “Thus Legendre's contribution lay in introducing a convenient notation that recorded whether a is a residue or a non-residue modulo p.”

**Sources:**

- [A.-M. Legendre, Essai sur la théorie des nombres, Paris (1798), p. 186](https://archive.org/details/essaisurlathor00lege)

### Treating 0 separately gives equal numbers of quadratic residues and nonresidues

**Remember:** Leave zero out, and residues are exactly half of the nonzero classes.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A15 (Power residues, reciprocity). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** An integer a is a quadratic residue modulo n when a ≡ x² mod n for some x. Modulo an odd prime p, the usual convention leaves 0 out, so residues and nonresidues are nonzero classes.

**Problem.** Modulo an odd prime p, there are (p − 1)/2 residues and (p − 1)/2 nonresidues. The product of two nonresidues is a residue, and a residue times a nonresidue is a nonresidue.

**How the definition helps.** Without 0, the residues are the squares in the cyclic group of nonzero classes (our own). So they form a subgroup of index 2 (our own). Then all the product rules hold at once, and the Legendre symbol is a group homomorphism to ±1 (our own). With 0 counted as a residue, there are (p + 1)/2 residues against (p − 1)/2 nonresidues. The rule that a residue times a nonresidue is a nonresidue also fails, since 0 times anything is 0 (our own). For composite moduli the nonzero classes do not form a group, and the rules can fail. Quadratic reciprocity, the hard theorem, is not touched by this convention (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Quadratic residue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadratic_residue). It says: “In this case, it is customary to consider 0 as a special case and work within the multiplicative group of nonzero elements of the field”

**Sources:**

- [K. Ireland and M. Rosen, A Classical Introduction to Modern Number Theory, 2nd ed., Springer, 1990](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-2103-4)

### Requiring f(1) = 1 makes multiplicative functions a group

**Remember:** Asking f(1) = 1 throws out only the zero function, which has no inverse.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A25 (Arithmetic functions; related numbers; inversion formulas). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** An arithmetic function f is multiplicative when f(1) = 1 and f(mn) = f(m)f(n) for all coprime positive integers m and n. The Dirichlet convolution of f and g is (f∗g)(n) = Σ f(d) g(n/d), summed over the divisors d of n.

**Problem.** Under Dirichlet convolution, the multiplicative functions form an abelian group. Its identity is ε, with ε(1) = 1 and ε(n) = 0 for n &gt; 1. For example, the Möbius function μ is the inverse of the constant function 1.

**How the definition helps.** The condition f(1) = 1 removes exactly the one function that has no inverse. The product rule for coprime m and n alone gives f(1) = f(1)², so f(1) is 0 or 1 (our own). If f(1) = 0, then f(n) = f(n)f(1) = 0 for every n (our own). A function has a Dirichlet inverse exactly when f(1) ≠ 0, so the zero function cannot be in a group. With f(1) = 1, the inverse can be built step by step from g(1) = 1, and it is again multiplicative. This group structure makes Möbius inversion a one-line argument (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Dirichlet convolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirichlet_convolution), [Multiplicative function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiplicative_function). It says: “every not constantly zero multiplicative function has a Dirichlet inverse which is also multiplicative. In other words, multiplicative functions form a subgroup of the group of invertible elements of the Dirichlet ring.”

**Sources:**

- [T. M. Apostol, Introduction to Analytic Number Theory, Springer, 1976](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-5579-4)

### Leaving 1 out of the primes makes factorization unique

**Remember:** 1 is the empty product, with no factors to equal, so it is not prime.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A41 (Primes), 11A05. arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** A prime number is a natural number p ≥ 2 whose only divisors are 1 and p. In unbiased form, which treats products of every length alike: a nonzero natural number n is prime if, whenever n = a₁ ⋯ aₖ for some k ≥ 0, n is equal to one of the factors aᵢ. The case k = 0 excludes 1, because 1 is the empty product and has no factors to be equal to.

**Problem.** The fundamental theorem of arithmetic says that every positive whole number is a product of primes in exactly one way, up to the order of the factors.

**How the definition helps.** Leaving 1 out lets the uniqueness statement hold with no exception. If 1 counted as a prime, then 6 = 2·3 = 1·2·3 = 1·1·2·3 would have infinitely many prime factorizations. With 1 excluded, the theorem is true as stated, and 1 is simply the empty product of primes. The unbiased definition excludes 1 automatically. The nLab calls this a case of a general principle: a trivial object is "too simple to be simple". The definition only makes the theorem statable; the proof of uniqueness (Euclid's lemma) is unchanged. The example with factors of 1 is our own illustration.

**nLab:** [prime number](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/prime+number), [too simple to be simple](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/too+simple+to+be+simple), [biased definition](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/biased+definition), [fundamental theorem of arithmetic](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/fundamental+theorem+of+arithmetic), [unique factorization monoid](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/unique+factorization+monoid). It says: “hence might look like it deserves to be counted as a prime number, too, this would break the uniqueness of this prime factorization.”

**Sources:**

- [Chris K. Caldwell, Angela Reddick, Yeng Xiong, Wilfrid Keller, The History of the Primality of One: A Selection of Sources, Journal of Integer Sequences 15 (2012), Article 12.9.8](https://cs.uwaterloo.ca/journals/JIS/VOL15/Caldwell2/cald6.html)

### Allowing the empty product lets prime factorization include the number 1

**Remember:** The number 1 is the product of no primes.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove.* MSC 11A51 (Factorization; primality), 11A05. arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** The product of an empty list of numbers is defined to be 1. The product of a list with one number is that number. A prime factorization of n is then any finite list of primes, possibly empty, whose product is n.

**Problem.** Every positive integer is a product of primes, and the list of primes is unique up to order.

**How the definition helps.** The empty product lets one statement cover every positive integer, with no exceptions. The number 1 is the product of the empty list, and a prime p is the product of the list (p). Without these conventions, the theorem needs separate cases for 1 and for the primes, as the page says. The proof by strong induction also loses its special base case. The real work still lies in uniqueness, through Euclid's lemma, which says that a prime dividing a product divides a factor. The induction and Euclid's lemma remarks are our own.

**Wikipedia:** [Empty product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empty_product). It says: “As another example, the fundamental theorem of arithmetic says that every positive integer can be written uniquely as a product of primes. However, if we do not allow products with only 0 or 1 factors, then the theorem (and its proof) become longer.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Requiring the last partial quotient above 1 makes continued fractions unique

**Remember:** Forbid a final 1, and every rational has exactly one continued fraction.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A55 (Continued fractions). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** A simple continued fraction \[a₀; a₁, …, aₙ\] is a₀ + 1/(a₁ + 1/(… + 1/aₙ)). Here a₀ is an integer and the other terms are positive integers. The canonical form also requires aₙ &gt; 1 when n ≥ 1.

**Problem.** Every rational number has exactly one canonical simple continued fraction. Its terms are the quotients of the Euclidean algorithm.

**How the definition helps.** The condition throws out the one way a finite continued fraction can be rewritten. Without it, every rational has two expansions, since \[a₀; …, aₙ\] = \[a₀; …, aₙ − 1, 1\]. For example, 3/2 = \[1; 2\] = \[1; 1, 1\] (our own). The condition aₙ &gt; 1 removes the longer one, and the Euclidean algorithm always gives the shorter one. For irrational numbers the expansion is infinite and unique with no extra condition. The theory of convergents and best approximation does not depend on this choice (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Simple continued fraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simple_continued_fraction). It says: “These two representations agree except in their final terms. In the longer representation the final term in the continued fraction is 1; the shorter representation drops the final 1, but increases the new final term by 1.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Banning trailing nines makes decimals unique

**Remember:** Use trailing zeros, never an endless tail of nines.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A63 (Radix representation; digital problems). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Write a nonnegative real as its integer part and a sequence of decimal digits. Pad terminating decimals with zeros. Disallow an infinite tail of nines and unnecessary leading zeros.

**Problem.** Every nonnegative real number has exactly one decimal representation under this rule.

**How the definition helps.** Without a tail of nines, later digits cannot cancel the difference at the first differing place (our own). For example, 0.4999… and 0.5000… represent the same number without the rule (our own). The work in existence lies in choosing digits by successive rounding down and showing the partial values converge (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Decimal representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decimal_representation). It says: “decimal representations with a trailing infinite sequence of 9 are sometimes excluded.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### No consecutive powers makes base-φ numerals unique

**Remember:** In base φ, forbid 11 and each integer has one numeral.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11A63 (Radix representation; digital problems), 11B39. arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let φ = (1 + √5)/2, the golden ratio. A base-φ numeral writes a number as a sum of distinct powers of φ, with digits 0 and 1. Bergman's standard form also forbids two consecutive powers, that is, the digit block 11.

**Problem.** Every non-negative integer has exactly one finite base-φ numeral in standard form.

**How the definition helps.** The rule removes the identity that makes numerals ambiguous (our own reason). Wikipedia gives that identity as φⁿ + φⁿ⁻¹ = φⁿ⁺¹, so 011 and 100 name the same number. The rule keeps only 100. Uniqueness also needs finiteness, because Wikipedia notes that 1 = 0.101010… is in standard form as well. This is the same mechanism as in Zeckendorf's theorem for Fibonacci numbers (our own). Integers need negative powers of φ, so finiteness of the numeral needs its own proof (our own). The paper studies a new variant that allows φ⁰ and φ¹ together.

**Wikipedia:** [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden<sub>ratio</sub><sub>base</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/https%3A//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_ratio_base). It says: “Despite using an irrational number base, when using standard form, all non-negative integers have a unique representation as a terminating (finite) base-φ expansion.”

**Paper or page:** [On the representation of the natural numbers by powers of the golden mean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.07544). It says: “Well known is the standard representation, introduced by George Bergman in 1957, where a unique representation is obtained by requiring that no consecutive powers φⁿ and φⁿ⁺¹ do occur in the representation.”

**Sources:**

- [Michel Dekking, Ad van Loon, On the representation of the natural numbers by powers of the golden mean, arXiv:2111.07544 (2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.07544)
- [George Bergman, A Number System with an Irrational Base, Mathematics Magazine (1957)](https://doi.org/10.2307/3029218)

### Place values give digit divisibility tests

**Remember:** Replace the base by 1 or −1 before adding the digits.

*True by definition. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11A63 (Radix representation; digital problems). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For an integer base b ≥ 2, read the digits of a nonnegative integer as polynomial coefficients. Start with the constant coefficient at the rightmost digit. Evaluating the polynomial at b gives the integer.

**Problem.** A base-b integer is divisible by b − 1 exactly when its digit sum is. It is divisible by b + 1 exactly when its alternating digit sum is.

**How the definition helps.** Place value turns a divisibility test on the whole digit string into a substitution in its polynomial. Our own generalization uses b ≡ 1 modulo b − 1 and b ≡ −1 modulo b + 1. Polynomial evaluation respects congruence, so those substitutions give the digit sum and the alternating digit sum. This replaces separate decimal tests with one calculation.

**Paper or page:** [Divisibility by 11](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Divisibility_by_11), [Divisibility by 9](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Divisibility_by_9). It says: “a divisibility test for 11 is achieved by alternately adding and subtracting the digits and taking the result modulo 11.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Reading decimals as series makes 0.999… equal 1

**Remember:** The series for 0.999… is geometric, and it sums to exactly 1.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A63 (Radix representation; digital problems), 40A05. arXiv math.HO, math.CA. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** A decimal b₀.b₁b₂b₃… denotes the sum b₀ + b₁/10 + b₂/100 + ⋯, where b₁, b₂, b₃, … are its digits. The sum is the limit of the sums of successively longer initial parts.

**Problem.** 0.999… = 1.

**How the definition helps.** The series definition turns 0.999… into a geometric series with ratio 1/10. Its sum is (9/10)/(1 − 1/10) = 1. A common proof instead multiplies 0.999… by 10 and subtracts 0.999… from the result. That proof needs a justification for arithmetic with infinite strings of digits (our own comparison). The series proof relies on the geometric sum formula, which uses the fact that (1/10)ⁿ tends to zero.

**Wikipedia:** [0.999...](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/0.999...). It says: “A common development of decimal expansions is to define them as infinite series.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Remainder order gives decimal periods

**Remember:** A remainder that returns starts the same digits again.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove.* MSC 11A63 (Radix representation; digital problems). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** In long division, use the current remainder to determine the next digit and remainder. Measure a repeating block by the first return to a remainder that is on the repeating part. The multiplicative order of 10 modulo m is the least positive exponent that makes its power congruent to 1.

**Problem.** Take a reduced fraction a/q between 0 and 1. Remove all factors of 2 and 5 from q, and suppose the result m is greater than 1. The decimal period is the multiplicative order of 10 modulo m.

**How the definition helps.** Multiplicative order measures exactly the first return of a remainder after the initial decimal digits. In our own proof, write q = dm, where d contains all factors of 2 and 5. Choose j so that d divides 10ʲ. After j digits the remainder is congruent to a × 10ʲ modulo q. It returns k digits later exactly when q divides a × 10ʲ × (10ᵏ − 1). Since a and 10 are coprime to m, this is equivalent to m dividing 10ᵏ − 1. Each remainder divided by q equals its decimal tail, so the least positive return time is the period.

**Wikipedia:** [Repeating decimal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repeating_decimal). It says: “the period equals r, where r is the multiplicative order of 10 mod n”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### The series definition makes repeating decimals rational

**Remember:** The repeated digits give a geometric series with a rational sum.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A63 (Radix representation; digital problems), 97F40. arXiv math.NT, math.HO. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define a decimal as the sum of its digits times their place values 1, 1/10, 1/100, and so on. A repeating decimal has a block of digits that repeats forever after a finite start.

**Problem.** Every repeating decimal names a rational number.

**How the definition helps.** The series definition makes the repeating part a geometric series with a rational sum. A block of k digits gives the ratio r = 10⁻ᵏ and a rational first term a. The geometric sum is a/(1 − r), and the finite start also gives a rational number. The usual digit-shift proof instead multiplies by 10ᵏ and subtracts after the repeating part starts. The series definition justifies that arithmetic and moves the work into the geometric sum formula and the laws for limits (our own comparison).

**Wikipedia:** [Repeating decimal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repeating_decimal). It says: “A repeating decimal can also be expressed as an infinite series. That is, a repeating decimal can be regarded as the sum of an infinite number of rational numbers.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### No consecutive Fibonacci numbers makes Zeckendorf sums unique

**Remember:** No two neighbours allowed, so each number has one Fibonacci decomposition.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11B39 (Fibonacci and Lucas numbers and polynomials and generalizations), 11A67. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Use the shifted Fibonacci numbers F₁ = 1, F₂ = 2 and Fₙ₊₁ = Fₙ + Fₙ₋₁. A Zeckendorf decomposition of N writes N as a sum of distinct terms Fₙ. No two of the terms may be next to each other in the sequence.

**Problem.** Every positive integer has exactly one Zeckendorf decomposition. The paper extends this to other linear recurrences, where a sum that obeys the rules is called a legal decomposition.

**How the definition helps.** Our own reason: the ban on neighbours blocks the rewrite Fₙ + Fₙ₊₁ = Fₙ₊₂, the main source of a second sum. Our own example: without the ban, 3 = F₃ = F₁ + F₂ has two decompositions. Our own note: the shift to F₁ = 1, F₂ = 2 also removes the repeated 1 of the usual sequence 1, 1, 2, 3. With the ban, the greedy method gives existence, and uniqueness is a short induction (our own summary). The paper shows that the step to other recurrences is not free. It states that one family of recurrences loses uniqueness of decompositions.

**Wikipedia:** [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeckendorf's<sub>theorem</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/https%3A//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeckendorf%27s_theorem). It says: “every positive integer can be represented uniquely as the sum of one or more distinct Fibonacci numbers in such a way that the sum does not include any two consecutive Fibonacci numbers”

**Paper or page:** [Generalizing Zeckendorf's Theorem to Homogeneous Linear Recurrences, I](https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.08455). It says: “Zeckendorf's theorem states that every positive integer can be written uniquely as the sum of non-consecutive shifted Fibonacci numbers {Fₙ}, where we take F₁=1 and F₂=2.”

**Sources:**

- [Thomas C. Martinez, Steven J. Miller, Clayton Mizgerd, Chenyang Sun, Generalizing Zeckendorf's Theorem to Homogeneous Linear Recurrences, I, arXiv:2001.08455 (2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.08455)

### The Fibonacci matrix turns index addition into matrix multiplication

**Remember:** Powers of Q add exponents, so Fibonacci indices add too.

*True by definition.* MSC 11B39 (Fibonacci and Lucas numbers and polynomials and generalizations), 11B37. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let Q be the 2 × 2 matrix with rows (1, 1) and (1, 0). For n ≥ 0, define Fₙ as the entry in row 2, column 1 of Qⁿ. The usual definition starts with F₀ = 0 and F₁ = 1 and adds the previous two terms to get the next.

**Problem.** For all m, n ≥ 1, Fₘ₊ₙ = FₘFₙ₊₁ + Fₘ₋₁Fₙ.

**How the definition helps.** The matrix definition turns addition of Fibonacci indices into the matrix law Qᵐ⁺ⁿ = QᵐQⁿ. For k ≥ 1, the rows of Qᵏ are (Fₖ₊₁, Fₖ) and (Fₖ, Fₖ₋₁). Comparing the entries in row 2, column 1 gives the stated formula (our own calculation). The work is to check the entries by multiplying by Q, which also recovers the usual recurrence. A proof from the recurrence alone can use induction on n with two starting cases (our own comparison).

**Wikipedia:** [Fibonacci sequence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fibonacci_sequence). It says: “Moreover, since AⁿAᵐ = Aⁿ⁺ᵐ for any square matrix A, the following identities can be derived”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Tile counts give Fibonacci binomial sums

**Remember:** Fix the number of long tiles and choose their places.

*True by definition.* MSC 11B39 (Fibonacci and Lucas numbers and polynomials and generalizations), 05A15. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For n ≥ 1, define Fₙ as the number of tilings of a strip of length n − 1. Use tiles of length one or two, and count the empty tiling once. The usual definition starts with F₁ = F₂ = 1 and adds two successive terms.

**Problem.** Let C(a,b) count the b-element subsets of an a-element set. For n ≥ 1, Fₙ is the sum of C(n−k−1,k) for k from 0 to ⌊(n−1)/2⌋.

**How the definition helps.** The tiling definition lets us count by the number of long tiles (our own). With k long tiles, there are n−1−k tiles altogether (our own). Choose k of those positions for the long tiles (our own). Splitting tilings by their last tile gives the usual recurrence with the same starting values (our own).

**Paper or page:** [Fibonacci Number as Sum of Binomial Coefficients](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Fibonacci_Number_as_Sum_of_Binomial_Coefficients). It says: “Fₙ = (n − 1 choose 0) + (n − 2 choose 1) + (n − 3 choose 2) + ⋯ + (n − j choose j − 1) + (n − j − 1 choose j)”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### With the Fibonacci matrix, Cassini's identity is a determinant

**Remember:** The determinant of Q is −1, so its n-th power has determinant (−1)ⁿ.

*True by definition.* MSC 11B39 (Fibonacci and Lucas numbers and polynomials and generalizations), 15A15. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let Q be the 2 × 2 matrix with rows (1, 1) and (1, 0). For n ≥ 0, define Fₙ as the entry in row 2, column 1 of Qⁿ. The usual definition starts with F₀ = 0 and F₁ = 1 and adds the previous two terms to get the next.

**Problem.** For every n ≥ 1, Fₙ₊₁Fₙ₋₁ − Fₙ² = (−1)ⁿ.

**How the definition helps.** The matrix definition makes Cassini’s identity a calculation of the determinant of Qⁿ in two ways. For n ≥ 1, its rows are (Fₙ₊₁, Fₙ) and (Fₙ, Fₙ₋₁), so its determinant is Fₙ₊₁Fₙ₋₁ − Fₙ². The determinant of Q is −1, and determinants multiply, so the determinant of Qⁿ is (−1)ⁿ. The work is to check the entries by multiplying by Q, which also gives the usual recurrence. Taking the matrix entry as the definition is our own framing of the published proof.

**Wikipedia:** [Fibonacci sequence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fibonacci_sequence). It says: “Taking the determinant of both sides of this equation yields Cassini's identity,”

**Paper or page:** [Cassini's Identity](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Cassini's_Identity). It says: “Let Fₖ be the kth Fibonacci number. Then: Fₙ₊₁ Fₙ₋₁ − Fₙ² = (−1)ⁿ”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Prime powers count factorial factors

**Remember:** Count multiples of p once more at every power of p.

*True by definition.* MSC 11B65 (Binomial coefficients; factorials; \\(q\\)-identities), 11A51. arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** For a prime p and a positive integer a, count the positive powers of p that divide a. This count equals the largest integer e for which pᵉ divides a.

**Problem.** For a positive integer n, the exponent of p in n! is the sum of ⌊n/pⁱ⌋ over i ≥ 1. The brackets mean to round down.

**How the definition helps.** The count definition lets us add the contributions of all factors of n! one power of p at a time (our own). The number of factors divisible by pⁱ is ⌊n/pⁱ⌋ (our own). Unique factorization ensures the exponent of a product is the sum of the individual exponents (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Legendre's formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legendre%27s_formula). It says: “Each multiple of p² contributes an additional factor of p, each multiple of p³ contributes yet another factor of p, etc.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Rotating subsets proves prime divisibility

**Remember:** A nontrivial subset on a prime-sized circle has a full set of rotations.

*True by definition.* MSC 11B65 (Binomial coefficients; factorials; \\(q\\)-identities), 05A19. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define C(p,k) as the number of k-element subsets of p labeled positions on a circle. Its usual formula is p!/(k!(p−k)!).

**Problem.** If p is prime and 0 &lt; k &lt; p, then p divides C(p,k).

**How the definition helps.** Counting subsets lets us group them into sets of p rotated copies (our own). Any nonzero shift visits all positions since p is prime (our own). A subset fixed by such a shift must therefore be empty or full (our own). The factorial formula follows by counting ordered selections and dividing by the number of orders of each subset (our own).

**Paper or page:** [Binomial Coefficient of Prime](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Binomial_Coefficient_of_Prime). It says: “∀ k ∈ ℤ: 0 &lt; k &lt; p: (p choose k) ≡ 0 (mod p)”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Primitive Pythagorean triples correspond to coprime pairs of opposite parity

**Remember:** Coprime m and n, one of them even, give each primitive triple once.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11D09 (Quadratic and bilinear Diophantine equations). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** A Pythagorean triple is three positive integers a, b, c with a² + b² = c². It is primitive when a, b and c have no common divisor larger than 1.

**Problem.** Take a primitive triple with b even. Then a = m² − n², b = 2mn and c = m² + n². Here m &gt; n &gt; 0 is a unique pair of coprime integers that are not both odd.

**How the definition helps.** The two conditions on m and n remove the only ways Euclid's formula repeats a triple or misses primitivity. If m and n share a factor k, the triple is k² times a smaller one (our own). If m and n are both odd, all three numbers are even. For example, m = 3 and n = 1 give (8, 6, 10), which is twice (3, 4, 5) (our own). With both conditions, the formula is a bijection onto the primitive triples. Every other triple is a multiple of exactly one primitive triple. The proof itself is a short factorization argument.

**Wikipedia:** [Pythagorean triple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythagorean_triple). It says: “Every primitive triple arises (after the exchange of a and b, if a is even) from a unique pair of coprime numbers m, n, one of which is even.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Complex norms show that sums of two squares multiply

**Remember:** Complex norms multiply, so sums of two squares multiply.

*True by definition.* MSC 11E25 (Sums of squares and representations by other particular quadratic forms). arXiv math.NT. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Use the complex norm N(z) = z z̄, where z̄ is the complex conjugate. For z = a + bi, this norm is a² + b².

**Problem.** For real a, b, c and d, (a² + b²)(c² + d²) = (ac − bd)² + (ad + bc)². In particular, products of sums of two integer squares are again sums of two integer squares.

**How the definition helps.** The norm turns the four-variable identity into the rule N(zw) = N(z)N(w). Unlike direct polynomial expansion, this proof uses the fact that conjugation preserves products (our own). The two squares come from the real and imaginary parts of zw.

**Wikipedia:** [Brahmagupta–Fibonacci identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmagupta%E2%80%93Fibonacci_identity). It says: “the Brahmagupta–Fibonacci identity is equivalent to the multiplicative property for absolute values of complex numbers”

**Paper or page:** [Brahmagupta-Fibonacci Identity](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Brahmagupta-Fibonacci_Identity). It says: “Let a, b, c, d be numbers. Then: (a² + b²)(c² + d²) = (ac + bd)² + (ad − bc)²”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Defining e as the factorial series gives a short irrationality proof

**Remember:** Scaled by n!, the tail is a positive integer below one.

*True by definition.* MSC 11J72 (Irrationality; linear independence over a field), 26A09. arXiv math.CA. Level 1: early undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define e as the sum of 1/n! over all n ≥ 0. Another common definition is the limit of (1 + 1/n)ⁿ.

**Problem.** The number e is irrational.

**How the definition helps.** With the series definition, the tail after n terms is explicit and small, which drives the proof. Suppose e = p/q, where q ≥ 2 because 2 &lt; e &lt; 3 (our own). Then x = q!(e − the sum of 1/k! for k ≤ q) is a positive integer. The tail is 1/(q + 1) + 1/((q + 1)(q + 2)) + …, so a geometric series gives x &lt; 1/q &lt; 1. This contradiction shows that e is irrational. The work moves into showing that the series and the limit give the same number, by the binomial theorem and a tail bound (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Proof that e is irrational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proof_that_e_is_irrational). It says: “the fast convergence of the series representation implies that x is still strictly smaller than 1.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Defining the gcd as a generator gives Bézout’s identity

**Remember:** The gcd lives in aℤ + bℤ, so it is some ax + by.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors), 13F07. arXiv math.NT, math.AC. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** For integers a and b, not both zero, let S be the set of all ax + by with integer x and y. Define gcd(a, b) as the positive d for which S consists of all integer multiples of d. The usual definition takes the largest positive integer that divides both a and b.

**Problem.** For integers a and b, not both zero, there are integers x and y with ax + by = gcd(a, b).

**How the definition helps.** The generator definition puts gcd(a, b) in S, so it is one of the combinations ax + by. The work is to prove that S consists of the multiples of its least positive element d. Dividing any member of S by d leaves a remainder in S, so the remainder must be zero. Also, d divides a and b, and every common divisor divides d because d is a combination of a and b. Thus d is the largest positive common divisor (our own explanation).

**nLab:** [greatest common divisor](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/greatest+common+divisor). It says: “From the point of view of principal ideals in a pid or Bézout domain R, the gcd corresponds to taking their join: (gcd(a, b)) = (a) + (b).”

**Wikipedia:** [Bézout's identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%A9zout%27s_identity). It says: “The reason is that the ideal Ra + Rb is principal and equal to Rd.”

**Paper or page:** [Bézout's Identity](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Bézout's_Identity). It says: “Furthermore, gcd{a, b} is the smallest positive integer combination of a and b.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### The divisibility definition makes the gcd associative

**Remember:** Greatest lower bounds of three elements do not care about brackets.

*True by definition. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11A05 (Multiplicative structure; Euclidean algorithm; greatest common divisors), 06B99. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Order the nonnegative integers by divisibility, so a comes below b if b = ak for some nonnegative integer k. Define gcd(a, b) as their greatest lower bound, meaning the common divisor that every common divisor divides. The usual definition takes the largest common divisor by size and needs a separate convention for gcd(0, 0).

**Problem.** For all nonnegative integers a, b, and c, gcd(a, gcd(b, c)) = gcd(gcd(a, b), c).

**How the definition helps.** The divisibility definition makes both groupings the greatest lower bound of the same three numbers (our own argument). A divisor of a and gcd(b, c) is exactly a common divisor of a, b, and c. The same reasoning applies to the other grouping. The work moves into proving that the greatest lower bounds exist. Bézout’s identity gives this for pairs other than (0, 0), since every common divisor divides every integer combination of the pair. For (0, 0), every number divides 0, so 0 itself is the greatest lower bound.

**nLab:** [greatest common divisor](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/greatest+common+divisor). It says: “The greatest common divisor of a, b is their meet in this lattice.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### The group of modular units gives Euler’s theorem

**Remember:** The order of an element divides the order of the group.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A07 (Congruences; primitive roots; residue systems). arXiv math.NT, math.GR. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let n ≥ 2 and define φ(n) as the size of the group of units modulo n. These are the residues with multiplicative inverses. The usual definition counts integers from 1 to n that are coprime to n.

**Problem.** If a is coprime to n, then a raised to the power φ(n) is 1 modulo n. When n is prime, this gives Fermat’s little theorem.

**How the definition helps.** The group definition lets Lagrange’s theorem show that the number of powers of a divides φ(n). Those powers form a subgroup of size k, where aᵏ is 1 modulo n. Since φ(n) is a multiple of k, raising a to φ(n) gives 1. The work moves into Lagrange’s theorem and into using Bézout’s identity to identify the invertible residues (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Euler's theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler%27s_theorem). It says: “Lagrange's theorem states that the order of any subgroup of a finite group divides the order of the entire group”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Euler’s power formula makes the Legendre symbol multiplicative

**Remember:** Powers of a product are products of powers.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A15 (Power residues, reciprocity). arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let p be an odd prime and let p not divide a. Define the Legendre symbol (a/p) as the sign congruent to a raised to (p − 1)/2 modulo p. The usual definition assigns +1 to nonzero squares modulo p and −1 to other nonzero residues.

**Problem.** If p does not divide a or b, then (ab/p) = (a/p)(b/p). In particular, the product of two nonsquares modulo p is a square.

**How the definition helps.** The power definition makes the symbol multiplicative because powers of products are products of powers. The work moves into Euler’s criterion, which shows that the power gives +1 exactly on nonzero squares. Fermat’s little theorem first shows that the power is ±1. The nonzero squares give all the roots of the degree (p − 1)/2 polynomial that tests for +1 (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Legendre symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legendre_symbol), [Euler's criterion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler%27s_criterion). It says: “By Euler's criterion, which had been discovered earlier and was known to Legendre, these two definitions are equivalent.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Permutation signs make the Jacobi symbol multiplicative in its numerator

**Remember:** Composing the permutations multiplies their signs.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A15 (Power residues, reciprocity), 20B30. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let n be odd and positive, and a coprime to n. Define the Jacobi symbol (a\|n) as the sign of the permutation that multiplies every residue modulo n by a. The sign is +1 for an even number of swaps and −1 for an odd number. The usual definition multiplies the Legendre symbols for the prime factors of n, counted with repetition.

**Problem.** For odd n &gt; 0 and a and b coprime to n, (ab\|n) = (a\|n)(b\|n).

**How the definition helps.** The permutation definition gives multiplicativity because multiplication by ab composes the two permutations, and signs multiply under composition (our own). The work moves into proving the two definitions agree. Zolotarev’s lemma gives the equality for prime n. For prime powers, one tracks the signs on the fibers of reduction modulo the previous power (our own). The Chinese remainder theorem combines the prime powers, since all the sets of residues have odd size (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Zolotarev's lemma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zolotarev%27s_lemma). It says: “a generalization of Zolotarev's lemma is that the Jacobi symbol above is the sign of this permutation.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### The Kronecker symbol matches primitive real characters with quadratic fields

**Remember:** Let the bottom of the symbol be any integer, and characters become symbols.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove.* MSC 11A15 (Power residues, reciprocity), 11R11, 11M06. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** The Jacobi symbol (a/n) is defined only for odd positive n. The Kronecker symbol extends it to every integer n. It adds fixed values for (a/2), (a/−1) and (a/0), and extends by multiplicativity in n.

**Problem.** For each nonzero D ≡ 0 or 1 mod 4, n ↦ (D/n) is a real Dirichlet character modulo \|D\|. It is primitive exactly when D is a fundamental discriminant, and these characters correspond one-to-one to quadratic fields. For a prime p, χ(p) is 1, −1 or 0 when p splits, stays prime, or ramifies in that field.

**How the definition helps.** With any integer allowed below, the discriminant D can sit on top, and n ↦ (D/n) becomes a character on all integers. The Jacobi symbol (D/n) is defined only for odd positive n. So it gives no values at even or negative n, which a character needs (our own). The Kronecker symbol fills in these values so that n ↦ (D/n) has period \|D\| (our own). So each quadratic field gets its character as one symbol, (D/·), with D the field discriminant. The page says that the value at a prime p records how p factors in the field. The hard part is quadratic reciprocity, which shows that (D/·) is periodic in its lower entry (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Kronecker symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kronecker_symbol). It says: “In particular, primitive real Dirichlet characters χ are in a 1–1 correspondence with quadratic fields F=ℚ(√m), where m is a nonzero square-free integer”

**Sources:**

- [L. Kronecker, Zur Theorie der elliptischen Funktionen, Sitzungsberichte der Preussischen Akademie der Wissenschaften zu Berlin (1885) 761–784](https://archive.org/stream/sitzungsberichte1885deutsch#page/760) (link not checked)
- [H. Davenport, Multiplicative Number Theory, 2nd ed., Graduate Texts in Mathematics 74, Springer, 1980](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-5927-3)

### Counting cyclic generators gives the totient divisor sum

**Remember:** Each element generates exactly one subgroup, so each is counted once.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A25 (Arithmetic functions; related numbers; inversion formulas). arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define φ(d) as the number of generators of a cyclic group of size d. A generator is an element whose powers fill the group. The usual definition counts the integers from 1 to d that are coprime to d.

**Problem.** For n ≥ 1, the sum of φ(d) over the positive divisors d of n is n.

**How the definition helps.** Counting cyclic generators makes the divisor sum count every element of one cyclic group exactly once. A cyclic group of size n has one subgroup of each size d dividing n. Each element generates exactly one such subgroup. The work moves into this subgroup count and into showing that the class of k generates ℤ/dℤ exactly when k is coprime to d.

**Wikipedia:** [Euler's totient function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler%27s_totient_function). It says: “φ(d) is also equal to the number of possible generators of the cyclic group”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Counting modular units makes Euler’s totient multiplicative

**Remember:** Units of a product ring are pairs of units.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A25 (Arithmetic functions; related numbers; inversion formulas), 11A07. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define φ(n) as the number of units in ℤ/nℤ, the ring of residues modulo n. A unit is a residue with a multiplicative inverse. The usual definition counts integers from 1 to n that are coprime to n.

**Problem.** For coprime positive integers m and n, φ(mn) = φ(m)φ(n).

**How the definition helps.** Counting units makes the product formula follow from the fact that a pair is invertible exactly when both entries are. The Chinese remainder theorem identifies residues modulo mn with pairs of residues modulo m and n, preserving addition and multiplication. The work moves into that theorem and into Bézout’s identity, which makes a residue invertible exactly when it is coprime to n (our own).

**nLab:** [multiplicative group of integers modulo n](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/multiplicative+group+of+integers+modulo+n). It says: “This is a corollary of the Chinese remainder theorem, which asserts that the canonical ring map ℤ/mn → ℤ/m × ℤ/n is an isomorphism if m, n are coprime.”

**Wikipedia:** [Euler's totient function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler%27s_totient_function). It says: “the group of units of the ring ℤ/nℤ”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Dirichlet convolution makes divisor power sums multiplicative

**Remember:** Convolving functions that multiply across coprime inputs preserves that property.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A25 (Arithmetic functions; related numbers; inversion formulas). arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** The Dirichlet convolution f ∗ g has value at n given by summing f(d)g(n/d) over all positive divisors d of n. Set σₖ = 𝟙 ∗ gₖ, where 𝟙 is the constant function 1 and gₖ(n) = nᵏ. Equivalently, σₖ(n) is the sum of the kth powers of the positive divisors of n.

**Problem.** If m and n are coprime positive integers, then σₖ(mn) = σₖ(m)σₖ(n). This includes the divisor count when k = 0.

**How the definition helps.** The convolution definition gives multiplicativity because both 𝟙 and gₖ multiply across coprime inputs. Instead of proving this separately for each divisor sum, prove that convolution preserves this property. The work moves into splitting each divisor of mn uniquely as a divisor of m times a divisor of n.

**Wikipedia:** [Dirichlet convolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirichlet_convolution), [Divisor function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisor_function). It says: “The Dirichlet convolution of two multiplicative functions is again multiplicative”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Primitive root sums make the Möbius divisor sum vanish

**Remember:** All nth roots of unity add to zero when n exceeds one.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A25 (Arithmetic functions; related numbers; inversion formulas), 11R18. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define μ(n) as the sum of the primitive nth roots of unity. These are the roots for which n is the least positive power that gives 1. The usual formula gives (−1)ᵏ for a product of k distinct primes, and zero if a prime square divides n.

**Problem.** The sum of μ(d) over all positive divisors d of n is 1 if n = 1 and 0 otherwise.

**How the definition helps.** The primitive-root definition makes the divisor sum the sum of all nth roots of unity. Each root has one order d dividing n, the least positive power that gives 1. For n &gt; 1 their sum is zero, since the coefficient of xⁿ⁻¹ in xⁿ − 1 is zero (our own). In the usual formula, each distinct prime dividing n contributes a factor of 1 − 1 to the divisor sum (our own). Both definitions give the same divisor sums, which determine their values by induction on n (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Möbius function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B6bius_function). It says: “each nth root of unity is a primitive dth root of unity for exactly one divisor d of n.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### The unit group exponent gives Carmichael’s least universal power

**Remember:** The group exponent is the least power that sends every element to one.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A25 (Arithmetic functions; related numbers; inversion formulas), 11A07. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** For n ≥ 2, define λ(n) as the exponent of the group of units modulo n. This is the least positive power that sends every invertible residue to 1. A formula instead computes λ on prime powers and takes their least common multiple.

**Problem.** Every residue a coprime to n raised to λ(n) is 1 modulo n. No smaller positive power works for all such a, and λ(n) divides φ(n). Here φ(n) counts the invertible residues modulo n.

**How the definition helps.** The group exponent gives the universal power and its minimality by definition. Lagrange’s theorem makes each element’s order divide the group size, so their least common multiple also divides it (our own). The work moves into showing that the formula gives the same exponent. The Chinese remainder theorem splits the group into its prime-power parts (our own). The formula then uses the structure of the unit group for each prime power (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Carmichael function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmichael_function). It says: “if λ(n) is considered as defined by the recurrence of the previous section, then it satisfies the property stated in the introduction”

**Sources:**

- [R. D. Carmichael, Note on a new number theory function, Bulletin of the American Mathematical Society 16 (1910), 232–238](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0002-9904-1910-01892-9)

### The product definition of primes makes factorization unique

**Remember:** A prime that divides a product divides a factor, which you can cancel.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A51 (Factorization; primality), 13F15. arXiv math.NT, math.AC. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Call an integer p prime if \|p\| &gt; 1 and p divides at least one factor whenever it divides a product. The usual divisor definition requires that p has no divisors except ±1 and ±p. An integer that meets the divisor definition and has \|p\| &gt; 1 is called irreducible.

**Problem.** If a nonzero integer factors into primes, the factorization is unique up to order and signs.

**How the definition helps.** The product definition lets the first prime on one side divide some prime on the other side (our own). Two primes with this divisibility relation differ at most by a sign. Cancel that pair and repeat (our own). The work moves into Euclid’s lemma, which proves the product rule from the usual divisor definition, using Bézout’s identity.

**Wikipedia:** [Euclid's lemma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclid%27s_lemma). It says: “It is used to define prime elements, a generalization of prime numbers to arbitrary commutative rings.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Matrix products give the determinant formula for continued fraction convergents

**Remember:** Each factor has determinant −1, so the product has determinant ±1.

*True by definition.* MSC 11A55 (Continued fractions). arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** In a simple continued fraction \[a₀; a₁, a₂, …\], a₀ is an integer and later terms are positive integers. A convergent hₙ/kₙ is the fraction cut off at aₙ. For each i from 0 to n, take the matrix with rows (aᵢ, 1) and (1, 0). Read hₙ, hₙ₋₁ and kₙ, kₙ₋₁ from the two rows of their ordered product.

**Problem.** For n ≥ 1, kₙhₙ₋₁ − kₙ₋₁hₙ = (−1)ⁿ. Thus hₙ and kₙ have no common divisor greater than one. Consecutive convergents differ by ±1/(kₙkₙ₋₁).

**How the definition helps.** The matrix product makes the left side the negative of its determinant. Each of the n + 1 factors has determinant −1. The work moves into showing that the matrices give the numerators and denominators of the usual nested fractions. Each matrix represents the map t ↦ aᵢ + 1/t, so composition builds the fraction (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Simple continued fraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simple_continued_fraction). It says: “has determinant (−1)ⁿ⁺¹, and thus belongs to the group of 2 × 2 unimodular matrices GL(2, ℤ).”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Reduced quadratic surds are exactly those with purely periodic continued fractions

**Remember:** Above one, with conjugate between minus one and zero, means it repeats immediately.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove.* MSC 11A55 (Continued fractions), 11R11. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** A quadratic surd is ζ = (P + √D)/Q with integers P, Q and D. Here D &gt; 0 is not a square, and Q divides P² − D. The surd is reduced, with conjugate η = (P − √D)/Q, when ζ &gt; 1 and −1 &lt; η &lt; 0.

**Problem.** The continued fraction of a quadratic surd repeats from its first term exactly when the surd is reduced. Then the expansion of −1/η repeats the same block in reverse order.

**How the definition helps.** The reduced condition is exactly what the continued fraction step keeps true, so it marks the surds on a cycle (our own). The step x ↦ 1/(x − ⌊x⌋) takes reduced surds to reduced surds (our own). There are finitely many reduced surds for each D, so the step must cycle, and the expansion repeats at once (our own). The golden ratio is reduced, and its expansion \[1; 1, 1, …\] repeats from the start. The square root of 2 is not reduced, since its conjugate is −√2. Its expansion \[1; 2, 2, …\] repeats only after the first term. From Galois's theorems one gets Lagrange's shape for √r, whose period ends in 2⌊√r⌋.

**Wikipedia:** [Periodic continued fraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periodic_continued_fraction). It says: “Galois proved that the regular continued fraction which represents a quadratic surd ζ is purely periodic if and only if ζ is a reduced surd.”

**Sources:**

- [É. Galois, Démonstration d'un théorème sur les fractions continues périodiques, Annales de Mathématiques pures et appliquées 19 (1828–1829) 294–301](https://www.numdam.org/item/AMPA_1828-1829__19__294_0/)

### The coefficient definition of binomial coefficients gives Lucas’s theorem

**Remember:** Modulo p, the base-p digits separate into independent polynomial factors.

*True by definition.* MSC 11B65 (Binomial coefficients; factorials; \\(q\\)-identities), 11A07. arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define C(m, n) as the coefficient of Xⁿ in (1 + X)ᵐ for nonnegative integers m and n. The coefficient is zero for n &gt; m. The other definitions count n-element subsets or use the formula m!/(n!(m − n)!) when n ≤ m.

**Problem.** Let p be prime and let mᵢ and nᵢ be the base-p digits of m and n. Then C(m, n) is congruent modulo p to the product of C(mᵢ, nᵢ) over the digit positions.

**How the definition helps.** The coefficient definition lets a polynomial factorization modulo p separate the binomial coefficient into its digit factors. Modulo p, (1 + X)ᵖ equals 1 + Xᵖ. For each digit mᵢ, set Yᵢ to X raised to pⁱ and take the factor (1 + Yᵢ) raised to mᵢ. Uniqueness of base-p digits lets only the choices nᵢ contribute to Xⁿ. The work moves into showing p divides C(p, k) for 0 &lt; k &lt; p, which the factorial formula gives.

**Wikipedia:** [Lucas's theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucas%27s_theorem). It says: “Comparing the coefficients of Xⁿ in the very first and last sum, we obtain Lucas's theorem.”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Generating functions make Bernoulli polynomial differences telescope to power sums

**Remember:** Shifting x changes the series so its difference has no denominator.

*True by definition.* MSC 11B68 (Bernoulli and Euler numbers and polynomials). arXiv math.NT, math.CO. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define Bₙ(x) as n! times the coefficient of tⁿ in the power series t exp(xt)/(exp(t) − 1). Another definition requires the integral of Bₙ from x to x + 1 to equal xⁿ.

**Problem.** For n ≥ 1, Bₙ(x + 1) − Bₙ(x) = nxⁿ⁻¹. For positive integers p and m, the sum of kᵖ from k = 0 to m − 1 equals (Bₚ₊₁(m) − Bₚ₊₁(0))/(p + 1).

**How the definition helps.** The series definition turns the difference at x + 1 and x into multiplication by exp(t) − 1, which cancels the denominator (our own). Comparing coefficients in t exp(xt) proves the difference formula. Summing it cancels all intermediate values and leaves only the two endpoints. To reconcile the definitions, integrate the series from x to x + 1 and compare the result with exp(xt) (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Bernoulli polynomials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernoulli_polynomials). It says: “Using either the above integral representation of xⁿ or the identity Bₙ(x + 1) − Bₙ(x) = nxⁿ⁻¹, we have”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Taking B₁ = +1/2 removes a sign exception from two formulas

**Remember:** Plus one half is what ζ(0) = −1/2 asks for.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11B68 (Bernoulli and Euler numbers and polynomials), 11B57. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** The Bernoulli numbers Bₙ are rational numbers that appear in formulas for sums of powers and for the Riemann zeta function ζ. Two conventions for them differ only at n = 1: B₁ = −1/2 or B₁ = +1/2. Older literature uses B₁ = +1/2, and Knuth has used it since 2022.

**Problem.** Two formulas should hold with no exception. The first is the sum-of-powers formula 1ᵐ + 2ᵐ + ⋯ + nᵐ = (1/(m+1)) Σₖ C(m+1, k) Bₖ nᵐ⁺¹⁻ᵏ. The second is Bₙ = −nζ(1 − n) for all n ≥ 1.

**How the definition helps.** The value B₁ = +1/2 lets both formulas hold with no special case at n = 1. With B₁ = −1/2, the sum formula for 1ᵐ + 2ᵐ + ⋯ + nᵐ needs a minus sign on the B₁ term, and Knuth wrote it with alternating signs. With B₁ = +1/2, the same formula has only plus signs. Also −1·ζ(0) = 1/2 = B₁, so the zeta formula holds at n = 1 too. For n ≥ 2 the two conventions agree, so only this one value changes. Our own remark: with B₁ = −1/2, the sum 0ᵐ + 1ᵐ + ⋯ + (n − 1)ᵐ has only plus signs. So each convention suits one form of the sum, and both stay in use.

**Wikipedia:** [Bernoulli number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernoulli_number). It says: “In the above Knuth meant B₁⁻; instead using B₁⁺ the formula avoids subtraction”

**Sources:**

- [D. E. Knuth, Johann Faulhaber and sums of powers, Mathematics of Computation (1993)](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0025-5718-1993-1197512-7)

### The generating function makes Bernoulli polynomial reflection one substitution

**Remember:** Replacing x by 1 − x does the same as replacing t by −t.

*True by definition. Confidence: medium.* MSC 11B68 (Bernoulli and Euler numbers and polynomials). arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Define Bₙ(x) as n! times the coefficient of tⁿ in t·exp(tx)/(exp(t) − 1). Another definition starts with B₀ = 1 and fixes each later Bₙ by two rules. Its derivative is n·Bₙ₋₁, and its integral over \[0, 1\] is zero.

**Problem.** For every n ≥ 0, Bₙ(1 − x) = (−1)ⁿBₙ(x).

**How the definition helps.** With the generating function, the identity is one line of algebra. Replacing x by 1 − x in t·exp(tx)/(exp(t) − 1) gives the same function as replacing t by −t. Comparing coefficients of tⁿ gives the sign (−1)ⁿ. With the derivative and integral rules, an induction also works, but it needs the uniqueness of the family. The work moves to showing that the generating function satisfies those rules, by differentiating in x and integrating over \[0, 1\]. These proof steps are our own; the ProofWiki page states the generating function.

**Paper or page:** [Generating Function for Bernoulli Polynomials](https://proofwiki.org/wiki/Generating_Function_for_Bernoulli_Polynomials). It says: “Then the generating function for Bₙ is:”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Excluding square n gives Pell's equation infinitely many solutions

**Remember:** Nonsquare n, so a fundamental solution generates them all.

*Rules out counterexamples.* MSC 11D09 (Quadratic and bilinear Diophantine equations), 11A55. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Pell's equation is x² − ny² = 1, with n a given positive integer that is not a perfect square. Its fundamental solution is the solution in positive integers with the smallest x.

**Problem.** For every nonsquare positive n, Pell's equation has a fundamental solution (x₁, y₁). All solutions in positive integers come from xₖ + yₖ√n = (x₁ + y₁√n)ᵏ.

**How the definition helps.** A square n makes the equation factor over the integers, and then only trivial solutions exist. If n = m², then (x − my)(x + my) = 1 forces both factors to be ±1 (our own). So the only solutions are (±1, 0). For nonsquare n, the continued fraction of √n is periodic, and one of its convergents gives the fundamental solution. In other words, the solutions are the units of norm 1 in ℤ\[√n\], and they form ±1 times a cyclic group (our own). The hard part is size: the page says the fundamental solution may have as many as about √n digits.

**Wikipedia:** [Pell's equation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pell%27s_equation). It says: “Joseph Louis Lagrange proved that, as long as n is not a perfect square, Pell's equation has infinitely many distinct integer solutions.”

**Sources:**

- [H. W. Lenstra Jr., Solving the Pell equation, Notices of the AMS 49 (2002) 182–192](https://www.ams.org/notices/200202/fea-lenstra.pdf) (link not checked)

### Pell solutions form a group as units of norm one

**Remember:** Norm-one elements multiply, and their conjugates give inverses.

*True by definition.* MSC 11D09 (Quadratic and bilinear Diophantine equations), 11R27. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** Let n be a positive integer that is not a square. Replace the integer pair (x, y) by x + y√n in the ring ℤ\[√n\]. Its norm is the product with its conjugate x − y√n, namely x² − ny². A Pell solution has norm one, so its conjugate is its multiplicative inverse.

**Problem.** The Pell solutions form a group under (x, y)(u, v) = (xu + nyv, xv + yu). The identity is (1, 0), and (x, −y) is the inverse of (x, y).

**How the definition helps.** The norm-one definition gives a group because norms multiply and conjugation gives inverses (our own). Instead of checking the group laws on pairs, use multiplication in ℤ\[√n\] (our own). The work moves into checking that conjugation preserves products, which follows from replacing √n by −√n (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Pell's equation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pell%27s_equation). It says: “a pair of integers (x, y) solves Pell's equation if and only if x + y√n is a unit with norm 1 in ℤ\[√n\].”

**Sources:** none listed. No primary source with a working link was found.

### Counting representations with order and signs gives Jacobi's four-square formula

**Remember:** Count every sign and every order, and the count is a divisor sum.

*Makes the theorem clean to state or prove.* MSC 11E25 (Sums of squares and representations by other particular quadratic forms), 11F27. arXiv math.NT. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** The number r₄(n) counts integer 4-tuples (x₁, x₂, x₃, x₄) with x₁² + x₂² + x₃² + x₄² = n. Order and signs both count, so r₄(1) = 8.

**Problem.** For n ≥ 1, r₄(n) is 8 times the sum of the divisors of n that are not divisible by 4. In particular, r₄(p) = 8(p + 1) for a prime p.

**How the definition helps.** Counting all integer vectors makes the counts the coefficients of a modular form (our own). The generating function Σ r₄(n) qⁿ is θ(q)⁴, with θ the theta series Σ q to the power m² over integers m (our own). This is a modular form of weight 2 for Γ₀(4), a space spanned by Eisenstein series (our own). So θ⁴ equals an Eisenstein series, whose coefficients are divisor sums (our own). Counting unordered sums of nonnegative squares would not give the coefficients of θ⁴, so this argument does not apply to that count (our own). The hard part is the theory of modular forms, or Jacobi's elliptic function identities (our own).

**Wikipedia:** [Jacobi's four-square theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobi%27s_four-square_theorem). It says: “Two representations are considered different if their terms are in different order or if the integer being squared (not just the square) is different”

**Sources:**

- [M. D. Hirschhorn, A simple proof of Jacobi's four-square theorem, Proc. Amer. Math. Soc. 101 (1987) 436–438](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0002-9939-1987-0908644-9)

### Quaternion norms show that sums of four squares multiply

**Remember:** Quaternion norms multiply, so sums of four squares multiply.

*True by definition.* MSC 11E25 (Sums of squares and representations by other particular quadratic forms), 11R52. arXiv math.NT, math.RA. Level 2: later undergraduate.

**Definition.** A quaternion has the form α = a + bi + cj + dk, with real coefficients. Its conjugate ᾱ reverses the signs of i, j and k. Define the squared norm N(α) = αᾱ, which equals a² + b² + c² + d².
